I’ve had stressful periods, but not something like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if it’s specific places exactly, but I’ve definitely started avoiding things I used to do because I feel too overwhelmed. I’m missing work deadlines, falling behind in my certificate classes, and I’ve stopped doing the routines that used to help me stay steady, like yoga, running, and cooking. It’s like I know those things would help, but I just can’t get myself to keep up with them.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there specific social situations or events that you've been avoiding because you fear something might go wrong or you might feel overwhelmed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I’ll be in bed for hours, laptop closed, but my mind is still going over work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments… it just doesn’t stop. And even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. I worry about normal things way more than I think I should. My mind jumps from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether school is a waste of money, to whether my family thinks I can’t handle things. Even small issues feel like they could mess up my whole future.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, nothing I would think of as a traumatic event. I’ve had stressful periods, but not something like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if it’s specific places exactly, but I’ve definitely started avoiding things I used to do because I feel too overwhelmed. I’m missing work deadlines, falling behind in my certificate classes, and I’ve stopped doing the routines that used to help me stay steady, like yoga, running, and cooking. It’s like I know those things would help, but I just can’t get myself to keep up with them.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific social situations or events that you've been avoiding because you fear something might go wrong or you might feel overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specific social situations. I don’t think I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid something embarrassing will happen or anything like that. It’s more that if something feels like too much to deal with, I’ll put it off or not go, but I can’t point to one type of event specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless, exactly. I’ve been stressed and worn down, but I haven’t noticed a deep sadness in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or anxiety that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel anxious a lot, but I haven’t noticed it coming on suddenly and peaking within a few minutes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot of the time it doesn’t even feel like there’s one clear reason. My mind just jumps around—from worrying I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family thinks I can’t handle things. Even small problems start feeling like they could mess up my whole future.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over, feeling like you can't control them?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t have like a ritual, but I get stuck on worries. I’ll try to tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then one worry turns into another. Before I know it I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over, instead of actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s a specific ritual I have to do, but I do feel pushed to check things when I’m worried. I’ll try to stop and focus on one thing, and then another worry pops up, and before I know it I’m going back through my calendar, my budget, or my assignments over and over instead of actually doing what I need to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because my sleep has really fallen apart. I’ll be in bed for hours, laptop closed, but my mind is still going over work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments… it just doesn’t stop. I worry about normal things way more than I think I should. I mean, nothing I would think of as a traumatic event. I’ve had stressful periods, but not something like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if it’s specific places exactly, but I’ve definitely started avoiding things I used to do because I feel too overwhelmed. I’m missing work deadlines, falling behind in my certificate classes, and I’ve stopped doing the routines that used to help me stay steady, like yoga, running, and cooking. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless, exactly. I’ve been stressed and worn down, but I haven’t noticed a deep sadness in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or anxiety that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel anxious a lot, but I haven’t noticed it coming on suddenly and peaking within a few minutes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I’m trying to calm myself down, but I just end up not getting anything done.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these repetitive checking behaviors are helping to reduce your anxiety?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Uncontrollable_Worry\nmanifestation: \"I try to tell myself to stop and focus on one thing, but one worry immediately leads to another until I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over instead of actually getting anything done.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I’ll tell myself to just focus on one thing, but then another worry comes up right away, and then I’m checking my calendar, my budget, my assignments over and over. It feels like I’m trying to calm myself down, but I just end up not getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checking behaviors are helping to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, but not really. I try to tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then another worry comes up right away. Then I’m checking my calendar, my budget, my assignments again and again, and I end up stuck instead of actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your calendar, budget, or assignments because you have an irresistible urge to do so?\n\nPatient: It does feel hard to resist, yeah. I’ll tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then another worry pops up right away. Before I know it, I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over, and I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these checking behaviors are intended to prevent something bad or unwanted from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really like I’m preventing one specific bad thing. It’s more that I can’t shut the worry off.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because my sleep has really fallen apart. I’ll be in bed for hours, laptop closed, but my mind is still going over work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments… it just doesn’t stop. And even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. I worry about normal things way more than I think I should. My mind jumps from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether school is a waste of money, to whether my family thinks I can’t handle things. Even small issues feel like they could mess up my whole future.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, nothing I would think of as a traumatic event. I’ve had stressful periods, but not something like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if it’s specific places exactly, but I’ve definitely started avoiding things I used to do because I feel too overwhelmed. I’m missing work deadlines, falling behind in my certificate classes, and I’ve stopped doing the routines that used to help me stay steady, like yoga, running, and cooking. It’s like I know those things would help, but I just can’t get myself to keep up with them.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific social situations or events that you've been avoiding because you fear something might go wrong or you might feel overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specific social situations. I don’t think I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid something embarrassing will happen or anything like that. It’s more that if something feels like too much to deal with, I’ll put it off or not go, but I can’t point to one type of event specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless, exactly. I’ve been stressed and worn down, but I haven’t noticed a deep sadness in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or anxiety that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel anxious a lot, but I haven’t noticed it coming on suddenly and peaking within a few minutes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot of the time it doesn’t even feel like there’s one clear reason. My mind just jumps around—from worrying I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family thinks I can’t handle things. Even small problems start feeling like they could mess up my whole future.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over, feeling like you can't control them?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t have like a ritual, but I get stuck on worries. I’ll try to tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then one worry turns into another. Before I know it I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over, instead of actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s a specific ritual I have to do, but I do feel pushed to check things when I’m worried. I’ll try to stop and focus on one thing, and then another worry pops up, and before I know it I’m going back through my calendar, my budget, or my assignments over and over instead of actually doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors, such as checking your calendar or budget, are something you must do to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I believe it prevents something bad from happening. It’s more like I feel uneasy if I don’t check, like I won’t be able to settle down. But I don’t have a clear thought like, “If I don’t do this, something terrible will happen.”\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that checking your calendar, budget, or assignments repeatedly helps to prevent something specific from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Not really something specific. It’s more that I can’t get the worry to stop. I’ll tell myself to just focus on one thing, but then another worry comes up right away, and then I’m checking my calendar, my budget, my assignments over and over. It feels like I’m trying to calm myself down, but I just end up not getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checking behaviors are helping to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, but not really. I try to tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then another worry comes up right away. Then I’m checking my calendar, my budget, my assignments again and again, and I end up stuck instead of actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your calendar, budget, or assignments because you have an irresistible urge to do so?\n\nPatient: It does feel hard to resist, yeah. I’ll tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then another worry pops up right away. Before I know it, I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over, and I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these checking behaviors are intended to prevent something bad or unwanted from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really like I’m preventing one specific bad thing. It’s more that I can’t shut the worry off. I try to stop and focus on one thing, but then another worry comes up, and I end up checking my calendar, budget, and assignments over and over instead of getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and anxiety started after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started after a big change. It’s been going on for about a year and three months, since I started an evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as an analyst. Since then, the worrying has just kind of stayed with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I try to stop and focus on one thing, but then another worry comes up, and I end up checking my calendar, budget, and assignments over and over instead of getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and anxiety started after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it started after a big change. It’s been going on for about a year and three months, since I started an evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as an analyst. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because my sleep has really fallen apart. I’ll be in bed for hours, laptop closed, but my mind is still going over work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments… it just doesn’t stop. And even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. I worry about normal things way more than I think I should. My mind jumps from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether school is a waste of money, to whether my family thinks I can’t handle things. Even small issues feel like they could mess up my whole future.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, nothing I would think of as a traumatic event. I’ve had stressful periods, but not something like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if it’s specific places exactly, but I’ve definitely started avoiding things I used to do because I feel too overwhelmed. I’m missing work deadlines, falling behind in my certificate classes, and I’ve stopped doing the routines that used to help me stay steady, like yoga, running, and cooking. It’s like I know those things would help, but I just can’t get myself to keep up with them.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific social situations or events that you've been avoiding because you fear something might go wrong or you might feel overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specific social situations. I don’t think I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid something embarrassing will happen or anything like that. It’s more that if something feels like too much to deal with, I’ll put it off or not go, but I can’t point to one type of event specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless, exactly. I’ve been stressed and worn down, but I haven’t noticed a deep sadness in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or anxiety that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel anxious a lot, but I haven’t noticed it coming on suddenly and peaking within a few minutes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot of the time it doesn’t even feel like there’s one clear reason. My mind just jumps around—from worrying I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family thinks I can’t handle things. Even small problems start feeling like they could mess up my whole future.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over, feeling like you can't control them?\n\nPatient: Yes, kind of. I don’t have like a ritual, but I get stuck on worries. I’ll try to tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then one worry turns into another. Before I know it I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over, instead of actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s a specific ritual I have to do, but I do feel pushed to check things when I’m worried. I’ll try to stop and focus on one thing, and then another worry pops up, and before I know it I’m going back through my calendar, my budget, or my assignments over and over instead of actually doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors, such as checking your calendar or budget, are something you must do to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I believe it prevents something bad from happening. It’s more like I feel uneasy if I don’t check, like I won’t be able to settle down. It’s more that I can’t get the worry to stop. I’ll tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then another worry pops up right away. Before I know it, I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over, and I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these checking behaviors are intended to prevent something bad or unwanted from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really like I’m preventing one specific bad thing. It’s more that I can’t shut the worry off. Before I know it I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over, instead of actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s a specific ritual I have to do, but I do feel pushed to check things when I’m worried. I’ll try to stop and focus on one thing, and then another worry pops up, and before I know it I’m going back through my calendar, my budget, or my assignments over and over instead of actually doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive behaviors, such as checking your calendar or budget, are something you must do to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I believe it prevents something bad from happening. It’s more that I can’t get the worry to stop. It feels like I’m trying to calm myself down, but I just end up not getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checking behaviors are helping to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, but not really. I try to tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then another worry comes up right away. Then I’m checking my calendar, my budget, my assignments again and again, and I end up stuck instead of actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your calendar, budget, or assignments because you have an irresistible urge to do so?\n\nPatient: It does feel hard to resist, yeah. I’ll tell myself to stop and just focus on one thing, but then another worry pops up right away. Before I know it, I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over, and I’m not actually getting anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these checking behaviors are intended to prevent something bad or unwanted from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really like I’m preventing one specific bad thing.
